It’s more like I’m trying to get this feeling to go away, and it doesn’t unless I check or mentally go through it again.\n\nDoctor: Are your repetitive checks or rituals specifically focused on preventing something bad from happening, or are they more about reducing a general feeling of anxiety or uncertainty?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of both, I think. Like, I tell myself I’m preventing something bad—if I reread the deployment plan one more time, or check the audit logs again, then I won’t miss the thing that causes a problem. Same with door locks or even old messages, like I’m making sure I didn’t leave something dangerous or wrong.\n\nBut if I’m honest, it’s also about the feeling. I’ll count through the steps in my head until they feel “safe,” which sounds… I don’t know, not very logical. And if I stop before it feels complete, I panic. I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone leaves doing that, rereading and checking, because walking away with that uncertainty feels unbearable.\n\nDoctor: Have these behaviors or rituals started to interfere with your relationships or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I mean, outside of work I used to have these routines that kept me, I don’t know, normal. Basketball with some guys, salsa nights with my wife and friends—well, she was always better at it than me, honestly, I mostly tried not to step on anyone—but I’ve stopped showing up. It’s like by the time I’m done checking or stuck trying to make a decision feel finished, I don’t have the time or the energy.\n\nAnd work has bled into it too. I missed this major cloud migration deadline because I just couldn’t sign off on decisions. I kept needing one more pass, one more confirmation. Now my team has started working around me instead of relying on me, which… that hurts. I used to be the person people came to for decisions. Now it feels like I’m becoming the bottleneck everywhere.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these thoughts and the need to repeat these actions cause you on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: It’s… a lot. Daily, honestly. What brings me in is that I keep getting these thoughts like, if I miss one setting, one access rule, one warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything. And I know, part of me knows that sounds extreme, because it’s not like every little checkbox is life or death, but in the moment it hits me like it is.\n\nIt’s like I’m personally responsible for catching every possible thing, and I can’t just move on from it. I’ll try to tell myself, okay, you checked, it’s fine, but then my brain goes, what if that was the one thing you missed? And then I’m right back in it. It’s exhausting. I mean, I used to be pretty good under pressure—my job kind of requires that—but this doesn’t feel like normal pressure anymore. It feels like I’m trapped in my own head most days.\n\nDoctor: Are your obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors only about preventing harm or are there other themes, such as symmetry or order, that also bother you?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s harm, I think. Like, it’s not really about things being lined up perfectly or the room needing to look a certain way—although my desk is a mess, which my wife used to joke was “organized chaos,” and honestly she wasn’t wrong.\n\nBut the thoughts that really grab me are the ones where I feel like if I miss one setting, one access rule, one warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything. And I know part of me understands that’s not always rational, but in the moment it feels like it’s on me personally, like I’m responsible for catching every possible thing. I can’t just let it go and move on.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other themes or concerns, besides preventing harm, that your obsessive thoughts or compulsive behaviors are focused on?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I keep trying to think if there’s some other category it fits into, but I don’t really notice another clear theme.\n\nI mean, I can get particular about things sometimes—my wife would say I overthink everything, including which takeout place to order from, and then the food gets cold while I’m reading reviews—but I don’t know if that’s the same thing you mean. The stuff that really hooks me, the stuff I can’t shake, seems to come back to preventing something bad from happening. Other than that, I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other themes or concerns, besides preventing harm, that your obsessive thoughts or compulsive behaviors are focused on?\n\nPatient: I mean, I keep circling back to the same thing, honestly. It’s not like I’m worried the pens aren’t straight or the dishes are in the wrong cabinet—though, actually, our kitchen cabinets are kind of a disaster because I unload the dishwasher in whatever way seems fastest, and then my wife can’t find the lids to anything.\n\nBut no, the thing that really grabs me is still that idea that if I miss one setting, one access rule, one warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything. And I know, sitting here, that sounds… too big, like my brain is making it bigger than it is. But when it hits, it feels like it’s my responsibility personally, and I can’t just move on from it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t think there’s another theme, at least not one I can really point to. I keep coming back to the same kind of thing, which is… if I miss one setting or one access rule or some warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything.

Not really, no. I keep trying to separate it out, like maybe there’s some other bucket it belongs in, but the thing that brings me here is that same loop: if I miss one setting, one access rule, one warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything.

And I know computers make everything sound dramatic—like, half the time a “critical alert” is just some software wanting attention, like a toddler with a keyboard—but in my head it doesn’t feel small. Even when part of me knows I’m being irrational, it feels like I’m personally responsible, like I can’t just move on unless I’m sure. So… no, I don’t think there are other thoughts that bother me in the same way. It’s mainly that.

I mean, it’s worst at work, because I’ll reread deployment plans and audit logs over and over, and then I can lose hours that way—like, literally staying after everyone’s gone because if I stop before it feels complete, I start to panic.\n\nBut at home it’s similar, just with different things. Door locks, old messages… I’ll go back and check them again, and then again. Sometimes I’ll count through the steps in my head, like if I can get the sequence to feel right or “safe,” then maybe I can move on.\n\nIt sounds ridiculous saying it out loud. I used to be the person who made careful lists for trips and things—actually, my wife used to joke that I packed like I was planning a server migration—but this isn’t really that. It’s more like I’m trying to get this feeling to go away, and it doesn’t unless I check or mentally go through it again.\n\nDoctor: Are your repetitive checks or rituals specifically focused on preventing something bad from happening, or are they more about reducing a general feeling of anxiety or uncertainty?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of both, I think. Like, I tell myself I’m preventing something bad—if I reread the deployment plan one more time, or check the audit logs again, then I won’t miss the thing that causes a problem. Same with door locks or even old messages, like I’m making sure I didn’t leave something dangerous or wrong.\n\nBut if I’m honest, it’s also about the feeling. I’ll count through the steps in my head until they feel “safe,” which sounds… I don’t know, not very logical. And if I stop before it feels complete, I panic. I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone leaves doing that, rereading and checking, because walking away with that uncertainty feels unbearable.\n\nDoctor: Have these behaviors or rituals started to interfere with your relationships or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I mean, outside of work I used to have these routines that kept me, I don’t know, normal. Basketball with some guys, salsa nights with my wife and friends—well, she was always better at it than me, honestly, I mostly tried not to step on anyone—but I’ve stopped showing up. It’s like by the time I’m done checking or stuck trying to make a decision feel finished, I don’t have the time or the energy.\n\nAnd work has bled into it too. I missed this major cloud migration deadline because I just couldn’t sign off on decisions. I kept needing one more pass, one more confirmation. Now my team has started working around me instead of relying on me, which… that hurts. I used to be the person people came to for decisions. Now it feels like I’m becoming the bottleneck everywhere.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these thoughts and the need to repeat these actions cause you on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: It’s… a lot. Daily, honestly. What brings me in is that I keep getting these thoughts like, if I miss one setting, one access rule, one warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything. And I know, part of me knows that sounds extreme, because it’s not like every little checkbox is life or death, but in the moment it hits me like it is.\n\nIt’s like I’m personally responsible for catching every possible thing, and I can’t just move on from it. I’ll try to tell myself, okay, you checked, it’s fine, but then my brain goes, what if that was the one thing you missed? And then I’m right back in it. It’s exhausting. I mean, I used to be pretty good under pressure—my job kind of requires that—but this doesn’t feel like normal pressure anymore. It feels like I’m trapped in my own head most days.\n\nDoctor: Are your obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors only about preventing harm or are there other themes, such as symmetry or order, that also bother you?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s harm, I think. Like, it’s not really about things being lined up perfectly or the room needing to look a certain way—although my desk is a mess, which my wife used to joke was “organized chaos,” and honestly she wasn’t wrong.\n\nBut the thoughts that really grab me are the ones where I feel like if I miss one setting, one access rule, one warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything. And I know part of me understands that’s not always rational, but in the moment it feels like it’s on me personally, like I’m responsible for catching every possible thing. I can’t just let it go and move on.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other themes or concerns, besides preventing harm, that your obsessive thoughts or compulsive behaviors are focused on?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I keep trying to think if there’s some other category it fits into, but I don’t really notice another clear theme.\n\nI mean, I can get particular about things sometimes—my wife would say I overthink everything, including which takeout place to order from, and then the food gets cold while I’m reading reviews—but I don’t know if that’s the same thing you mean. The stuff that really hooks me, the stuff I can’t shake, seems to come back to preventing something bad from happening. Other than that, I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other themes or concerns, besides preventing harm, that your obsessive thoughts or compulsive behaviors are focused on?\n\nPatient: I mean, I keep circling back to the same thing, honestly. It’s not like I’m worried the pens aren’t straight or the dishes are in the wrong cabinet—though, actually, our kitchen cabinets are kind of a disaster because I unload the dishwasher in whatever way seems fastest, and then my wife can’t find the lids to anything.\n\nBut no, the thing that really grabs me is still that idea that if I miss one setting, one access rule, one warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything. And I know, sitting here, that sounds… too big, like my brain is making it bigger than it is. But when it hits, it feels like it’s my responsibility personally, and I can’t just move on from it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other themes or concerns, besides preventing harm, that you think about repeatedly or feel compelled to act on?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s another theme, at least not one I can really point to. I keep coming back to the same kind of thing, which is… if I miss one setting or one access rule or some warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything.\n\nAnd I know, when I say it out loud, it sounds extreme. Like, I used to manage systems where there were a hundred little permissions menus, and honestly half of them look like they were designed by someone who hates people. Sorry, that’s not really the point. The point is, even when part of me knows I’m probably making it bigger than it is, it hits me like it’s personally on me to catch it. Like if I move on, I’m choosing to let something bad happen. So I don’t really think it’s symmetry or order or anything like that—it’s that responsibility feeling.\n\nDoctor: Are there any obsessive thoughts that bother you which are not related to preventing harm or danger?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I keep trying to separate it out, like maybe there’s some other bucket it belongs in, but the thing that brings me here is that same loop: if I miss one setting, one access rule, one warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything.\n\nAnd I know computers make everything sound dramatic—like, half the time a “critical alert” is just some software wanting attention, like a toddler with a keyboard—but in my head it doesn’t feel small. Even when part of me knows I’m being irrational, it feels like I’m personally responsible, like I can’t just move on unless I’m sure. So… no, I don’t think there are other thoughts that bother me in the same way. It’s mainly that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I keep trying to separate it out, like maybe there’s some other bucket it belongs in, but the thing that brings me here is that same loop: if I miss one setting, one access rule, one warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything.\n\nAnd I know computers make everything sound dramatic—like, half the time a “critical alert” is just some software wanting attention, like a toddler with a keyboard—but in my head it doesn’t feel small. Even when part of me knows I’m being irrational, it feels like I’m personally responsible, like I can’t just move on unless I’m sure. So… no, I don’t think there are other thoughts that bother me in the same way. It’s mainly that.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre your obsessive thoughts ever about something other than preventing harm or danger?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 43 / computer_or_information_systems_manager / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Obsessions\nmanifestation: \"What brings me in is that I keep getting these thoughts that if I miss one setting, one access rule, one warning sign, someone could get hurt or lose everything, and even though part of me knows it’s irrational, it hits me like I’m personally responsible and I can’t just move on.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think so. Or at least, not in the way that really gets stuck.

It’s always this same kind of thing—like I missed one setting, or an access rule, or some warning sign, and then somebody gets hurt or loses everything. And I know that sounds… I mean, computers have settings buried inside settings, it’s ridiculous, like you need a map just to find a checkbox sometimes—but when it hits me, it doesn’t feel like a little checkbox. It feels like if I don’t catch it, it’s on me personally.

So, no, I wouldn’t say it’s about other stuff. It’s mostly that responsibility feeling, like I can’t move on unless I’m sure.

---------- turn 16 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------
========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I’m here because I can’t seem to get past these thoughts that if I miss one thing—like one setting, one access rule, one warning sign—then someone could get hurt or lose everything. I work with systems, so details matter, obviously, and I know that, but this feels… bigger than normal responsibility.\n\nLike, part of me knows it doesn’t make sense all the time, or that I’m probably overestimating it, but it hits me like it’s completely on me. Like if I move on and something happens, then I caused it. And then I just can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or rituals to try and reduce these thoughts or the anxiety they cause?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean, I don’t call them rituals exactly, but… yes. I’ll reread deployment plans again and again, and audit logs, and then sometimes it’s not even work things—like I’ll check door locks or go back through old messages to make sure I didn’t miss something obvious.\n\nAnd I’ll sort of count through steps in my head, like, “Okay, did I do this, did I do that,” and I keep going until it feels… safe, I guess. Which is ridiculous because safe isn’t really a measurable thing, you know? In systems we have checklists for a reason, and I’m always telling my team to document properly, but then I’ll be the one still sitting there hours after everyone’s gone home because I can’t stop before it feels complete. If I try to leave too soon, I just panic.\n\nDoctor: Are these repeated actions or rituals you perform specifically to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes… yeah, that’s what it feels like. I reread deployment plans and audit logs because I’m trying to make sure I didn’t miss the thing that causes some disaster later. Same with door locks, or even old messages sometimes, which sounds unrelated, but in my head it’s all like… evidence that I either did or didn’t catch something.\n\nI’ll count through the steps in my head until it feels safe or complete. And I know “safe” isn’t a real stopping point—I mean, at work we have actual criteria for that—but I’ll still stay at the office for hours after everyone else has left because if I stop too early, I panic, like I’m letting something bad happen.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about missing something or causing harm feel like they are coming from outside of your own thinking, or are they more like your own worries?\n\nPatient: No, it doesn’t feel like a voice or something outside me, if that’s what you mean. It feels like my own worry, my own thinking… just turned up way too loud.\n\nLike I’ll be looking at a setting or an access rule, and suddenly it’s, “If you miss this, someone could get hurt, or everything could fall apart,” and then I’m stuck there. And I know systems work always has risk—I mean, that’s the whole job, managing risk—but this feels different. It feels personal, like if I move on before I’m absolutely sure, then I’m responsible for whatever happens.\n\nSo… yeah, it’s coming from me. I know that. I just can’t make it feel less real in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Are these worries and repetitive behaviors limited to your work with systems or do they also involve other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: It’s not just work, no. I mean, work is where it gets the worst because there are actual systems and logs and deployment plans, and if something breaks, everyone knows, you know? So I’ll reread those over and over.\n\nBut it spills out into other stuff too. Door locks, old messages… I’ll go back and check them again and again, and then I’ll count through steps in my head until it feels “safe,” whatever that means. It’s like when I used to make these elaborate checklists for travel—sorry, that’s not really the point—but I get stuck in that same loop.\n\nAnd at the office I’ve stayed hours after everyone leaves because I can’t stop before it feels complete. If I try to just walk away, I panic.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about missing something or causing harm ever pop into your mind unexpectedly, without you intentionally thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes, they do. It’s not like I sit down and decide to worry about it. I’ll just be doing something—looking at a setting, or honestly sometimes not even actively working on it—and this thought kind of drops in: what if I missed one access rule, one warning sign, one tiny thing, and someone gets hurt or loses everything because of me?\n\nAnd then I know, on some level, that’s not a reasonable way to think about every single detail. I manage systems, not… I don’t know, it’s not like I’m personally holding the whole world together. But in the moment it feels that way. It hits like I’m responsible, and then I can’t just move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these repetitive behaviors or mental acts in response to your worries about causing harm in areas of your life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… outside work too. I mean, it’s worst at work, because I’ll reread deployment plans and audit logs over and over, and then I can lose hours that way—like, literally staying after everyone’s gone because if I stop before it feels complete, I start to panic.\n\nBut at home it’s similar, just with different things. Door locks, old messages… I’ll go back and check them again, and then again. Sometimes I’ll count through the steps in my head, like if I can get the sequence to feel right or “safe,” then maybe I can move on.\n\nIt sounds ridiculous saying it out loud. I used to be the person who made careful lists for trips and things—actually, my wife used to joke that I packed like I was planning a server migration—but this isn’t really that. It’s more like I’m trying to get this feeling to go away, and it doesn’t unless I check or mentally go through it again.\n\nDoctor: Are your repetitive checks or rituals specifically focused on preventing something bad from happening, or are they more about reducing a general feeling of anxiety or uncertainty?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of both, I think. Like, I tell myself I’m preventing something bad—if I reread the deployment plan one more time, or check the audit logs again, then I won’t miss the thing that causes a problem. Same with door locks or even old messages, like I’m making sure I didn’t leave something dangerous or wrong.\n\nBut if I’m honest, it’s also about the feeling. I’ll count through the steps in my head until they feel “safe,” which sounds… I don’t know, not very logical. And if I stop before it feels complete, I panic. I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone leaves doing that, rereading and checking, because walking away with that uncertainty feels unbearable.\n\nDoctor: Have these behaviors or rituals started to interfere with your relationships or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I mean, outside of work I used to have these routines that kept me, I don’t know, normal. Basketball with some guys, salsa nights with my wife and friends—well, she was always better at it than me, honestly, I mostly tried not to step on anyone—but I’ve stopped showing up. It’s like by the time I’m done checking or stuck trying to make a decision feel finished, I don’t have the time or the energy.\n\nAnd work has bled into it too. I missed this major cloud migration deadline because I just couldn’t sign off on decisions. I kept needing one more pass, one more confirmation. Now my team has started working around me instead of relying on me, which… that hurts. I used to be the person people came to for decisions. Now it feels like I’m becoming the bottleneck everywhere.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these thoughts and the need to repeat these actions cause you on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: It’s… a lot. Daily, honestly. What brings me in is that I keep getting these thoughts like, if I miss one setting, one access rule, one warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything. And I know, part of me knows that sounds extreme, because it’s not like every little checkbox is life or death, but in the moment it hits me like it is.\n\nIt’s like I’m personally responsible for catching every possible thing, and I can’t just move on from it. I’ll try to tell myself, okay, you checked, it’s fine, but then my brain goes, what if that was the one thing you missed? And then I’m right back in it. It’s exhausting. I mean, I used to be pretty good under pressure—my job kind of requires that—but this doesn’t feel like normal pressure anymore. It feels like I’m trapped in my own head most days.\n\nDoctor: Are your obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors only about preventing harm or are there other themes, such as symmetry or order, that also bother you?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s harm, I think. Like, it’s not really about things being lined up perfectly or the room needing to look a certain way—although my desk is a mess, which my wife used to joke was “organized chaos,” and honestly she wasn’t wrong.\n\nBut the thoughts that really grab me are the ones where I feel like if I miss one setting, one access rule, one warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything. And I know part of me understands that’s not always rational, but in the moment it feels like it’s on me personally, like I’m responsible for catching every possible thing. I can’t just let it go and move on.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other themes or concerns, besides preventing harm, that your obsessive thoughts or compulsive behaviors are focused on?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I keep trying to think if there’s some other category it fits into, but I don’t really notice another clear theme.\n\nI mean, I can get particular about things sometimes—my wife would say I overthink everything, including which takeout place to order from, and then the food gets cold while I’m reading reviews—but I don’t know if that’s the same thing you mean. The stuff that really hooks me, the stuff I can’t shake, seems to come back to preventing something bad from happening. Other than that, I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other themes or concerns, besides preventing harm, that your obsessive thoughts or compulsive behaviors are focused on?\n\nPatient: I mean, I keep circling back to the same thing, honestly. It’s not like I’m worried the pens aren’t straight or the dishes are in the wrong cabinet—though, actually, our kitchen cabinets are kind of a disaster because I unload the dishwasher in whatever way seems fastest, and then my wife can’t find the lids to anything.\n\nBut no, the thing that really grabs me is still that idea that if I miss one setting, one access rule, one warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything. And I know, sitting here, that sounds… too big, like my brain is making it bigger than it is. But when it hits, it feels like it’s my responsibility personally, and I can’t just move on from it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other themes or concerns, besides preventing harm, that you think about repeatedly or feel compelled to act on?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s another theme, at least not one I can really point to. I keep coming back to the same kind of thing, which is… if I miss one setting or one access rule or some warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything.\n\nAnd I know, when I say it out loud, it sounds extreme. Like, I used to manage systems where there were a hundred little permissions menus, and honestly half of them look like they were designed by someone who hates people. Sorry, that’s not really the point. The point is, even when part of me knows I’m probably making it bigger than it is, it hits me like it’s personally on me to catch it. Like if I move on, I’m choosing to let something bad happen. So I don’t really think it’s symmetry or order or anything like that—it’s that responsibility feeling.\n\nDoctor: Are there any obsessive thoughts that bother you which are not related to preventing harm or danger?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I keep trying to separate it out, like maybe there’s some other bucket it belongs in, but the thing that brings me here is that same loop: if I miss one setting, one access rule, one warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything.\n\nAnd I know computers make everything sound dramatic—like, half the time a “critical alert” is just some software wanting attention, like a toddler with a keyboard—but in my head it doesn’t feel small. Even when part of me knows I’m being irrational, it feels like I’m personally responsible, like I can’t just move on unless I’m sure. So… no, I don’t think there are other thoughts that bother me in the same way. It’s mainly that.\n\nDoctor: Are your obsessive thoughts ever about something other than preventing harm or danger?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Or at least, not in the way that really gets stuck.\n\nIt’s always this same kind of thing—like I missed one setting, or an access rule, or some warning sign, and then somebody gets hurt or loses everything. And I know that sounds… I mean, computers have settings buried inside settings, it’s ridiculous, like you need a map just to find a checkbox sometimes—but when it hits me, it doesn’t feel like a little checkbox. It feels like if I don’t catch it, it’s on me personally.\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t say it’s about other stuff. It’s mostly that responsibility feeling, like I can’t move on unless I’m sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {

No, not really. I mean, I keep circling back to the same thing, and I know I’ve probably said it a few different ways already.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I’m here because I can’t seem to get past these thoughts that if I miss one thing—like one setting, one access rule, one warning sign—then someone could get hurt or lose everything. I work with systems, so details matter, obviously, and I know that, but this feels… bigger than normal responsibility.\n\nLike, part of me knows it doesn’t make sense all the time, or that I’m probably overestimating it, but it hits me like it’s completely on me. Like if I move on and something happens, then I caused it. And then I just can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or rituals to try and reduce these thoughts or the anxiety they cause?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean, I don’t call them rituals exactly, but… yes. I’ll reread deployment plans again and again, and audit logs, and then sometimes it’s not even work things—like I’ll check door locks or go back through old messages to make sure I didn’t miss something obvious.\n\nAnd I’ll sort of count through steps in my head, like, “Okay, did I do this, did I do that,” and I keep going until it feels… safe, I guess. Which is ridiculous because safe isn’t really a measurable thing, you know? If I try to leave too soon, I just panic.\n\nDoctor: Are these repeated actions or rituals you perform specifically to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes… yeah, that’s what it feels like. I reread deployment plans and audit logs because I’m trying to make sure I didn’t miss the thing that causes some disaster later. Same with door locks, or even old messages sometimes, which sounds unrelated, but in my head it’s all like… evidence that I either did or didn’t catch something.\n\nI’ll count through the steps in my head until it feels safe or complete. And I know “safe” isn’t a real stopping point—I mean, at work we have actual criteria for that—but I’ll still stay at the office for hours after everyone else has left because if I stop too early, I panic, like I’m letting something bad happen.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about missing something or causing harm feel like they are coming from outside of your own thinking, or are they more like your own worries?\n\nPatient: No, it doesn’t feel like a voice or something outside me, if that’s what you mean. It feels like my own worry, my own thinking… just turned up way too loud.\n\nLike I’ll be looking at a setting or an access rule, and suddenly it’s, “If you miss this, someone could get hurt, or everything could fall apart,” and then I’m stuck there. And I know systems work always has risk—I mean, that’s the whole job, managing risk—but this feels different. It feels personal, like if I move on before I’m absolutely sure, then I’m responsible for whatever happens.\n\nSo… yeah, it’s coming from me. I know that. I just can’t make it feel less real in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Are these worries and repetitive behaviors limited to your work with systems or do they also involve other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: It’s not just work, no. I mean, work is where it gets the worst because there are actual systems and logs and deployment plans, and if something breaks, everyone knows, you know? So I’ll reread those over and over.\n\nBut it spills out into other stuff too. Door locks, old messages… I’ll go back and check them again and again, and then I’ll count through steps in my head until it feels “safe,” whatever that means. It’s like when I used to make these elaborate checklists for travel—sorry, that’s not really the point—but I get stuck in that same loop.\n\nAnd at the office I’ve stayed hours after everyone leaves because I can’t stop before it feels complete. If I try to just walk away, I panic.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about missing something or causing harm ever pop into your mind unexpectedly, without you intentionally thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes, they do. It’s not like I sit down and decide to worry about it. I’ll just be doing something—looking at a setting, or honestly sometimes not even actively working on it—and this thought kind of drops in: what if I missed one access rule, one warning sign, one tiny thing, and someone gets hurt or loses everything because of me?\n\nAnd then I know, on some level, that’s not a reasonable way to think about every single detail. I manage systems, not… I don’t know, it’s not like I’m personally holding the whole world together. But in the moment it feels that way. It hits like I’m responsible, and then I can’t just move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these repetitive behaviors or mental acts in response to your worries about causing harm in areas of your life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… outside work too. I mean, it’s worst at work, because I’ll reread deployment plans and audit logs over and over, and then I can lose hours that way—like, literally staying after everyone’s gone because if I stop before it feels complete, I start to panic.\n\nBut at home it’s similar, just with different things. Door locks, old messages… I’ll go back and check them again, and then again. Sometimes I’ll count through the steps in my head, like if I can get the sequence to feel right or “safe,” then maybe I can move on.\n\nIt sounds ridiculous saying it out loud. I used to be the person who made careful lists for trips and things—actually, my wife used to joke that I packed like I was planning a server migration—but this isn’t really that. It’s more like I’m trying to get this feeling to go away, and it doesn’t unless I check or mentally go through it again.\n\nDoctor: Are your repetitive checks or rituals specifically focused on preventing something bad from happening, or are they more about reducing a general feeling of anxiety or uncertainty?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of both, I think. Like, I tell myself I’m preventing something bad—if I reread the deployment plan one more time, or check the audit logs again, then I won’t miss the thing that causes a problem. Same with door locks or even old messages, like I’m making sure I didn’t leave something dangerous or wrong.\n\nBut if I’m honest, it’s also about the feeling. I’ll count through the steps in my head until they feel “safe,” which sounds… I don’t know, not very logical. And if I stop before it feels complete, I panic. I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone leaves doing that, rereading and checking, because walking away with that uncertainty feels unbearable.\n\nDoctor: Have these behaviors or rituals started to interfere with your relationships or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I mean, outside of work I used to have these routines that kept me, I don’t know, normal. Basketball with some guys, salsa nights with my wife and friends—well, she was always better at it than me, honestly, I mostly tried not to step on anyone—but I’ve stopped showing up. It’s like by the time I’m done checking or stuck trying to make a decision feel finished, I don’t have the time or the energy.\n\nAnd work has bled into it too. I missed this major cloud migration deadline because I just couldn’t sign off on decisions. I kept needing one more pass, one more confirmation. Now my team has started working around me instead of relying on me, which… that hurts. I used to be the person people came to for decisions. Now it feels like I’m becoming the bottleneck everywhere.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these thoughts and the need to repeat these actions cause you on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: It’s… a lot. Daily, honestly. What brings me in is that I keep getting these thoughts like, if I miss one setting, one access rule, one warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything. And I know, part of me knows that sounds extreme, because it’s not like every little checkbox is life or death, but in the moment it hits me like it is.\n\nIt’s like I’m personally responsible for catching every possible thing, and I can’t just move on from it. I’ll try to tell myself, okay, you checked, it’s fine, but then my brain goes, what if that was the one thing you missed? And then I’m right back in it. It’s exhausting. I mean, I used to be pretty good under pressure—my job kind of requires that—but this doesn’t feel like normal pressure anymore. It feels like I’m trapped in my own head most days.\n\nDoctor: Are your obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors only about preventing harm or are there other themes, such as symmetry or order, that also bother you?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s harm, I think. Like, it’s not really about things being lined up perfectly or the room needing to look a certain way—although my desk is a mess, which my wife used to joke was “organized chaos,” and honestly she wasn’t wrong.\n\nBut the thoughts that really grab me are the ones where I feel like if I miss one setting, one access rule, one warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything. And I know part of me understands that’s not always rational, but in the moment it feels like it’s on me personally, like I’m responsible for catching every possible thing. I can’t just let it go and move on.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other themes or concerns, besides preventing harm, that your obsessive thoughts or compulsive behaviors are focused on?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I keep trying to think if there’s some other category it fits into, but I don’t really notice another clear theme.\n\nI mean, I can get particular about things sometimes—my wife would say I overthink everything, including which takeout place to order from, and then the food gets cold while I’m reading reviews—but I don’t know if that’s the same thing you mean. The stuff that really hooks me, the stuff I can’t shake, seems to come back to preventing something bad from happening. Other than that, I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other themes or concerns, besides preventing harm, that your obsessive thoughts or compulsive behaviors are focused on?\n\nPatient: I mean, I keep circling back to the same thing, honestly. It’s not like I’m worried the pens aren’t straight or the dishes are in the wrong cabinet—though, actually, our kitchen cabinets are kind of a disaster because I unload the dishwasher in whatever way seems fastest, and then my wife can’t find the lids to anything.\n\nBut no, the thing that really grabs me is still that idea that if I miss one setting, one access rule, one warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything. And I know, sitting here, that sounds… too big, like my brain is making it bigger than it is. But when it hits, it feels like it’s my responsibility personally, and I can’t just move on from it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other themes or concerns, besides preventing harm, that you think about repeatedly or feel compelled to act on?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s another theme, at least not one I can really point to. I keep coming back to the same kind of thing, which is… if I miss one setting or one access rule or some warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything.\n\nAnd I know, when I say it out loud, it sounds extreme. Like, I used to manage systems where there were a hundred little permissions menus, and honestly half of them look like they were designed by someone who hates people. Sorry, that’s not really the point. The point is, even when part of me knows I’m probably making it bigger than it is, it hits me like it’s personally on me to catch it. Like if I move on, I’m choosing to let something bad happen. So I don’t really think it’s symmetry or order or anything like that—it’s that responsibility feeling.\n\nDoctor: Are there any obsessive thoughts that bother you which are not related to preventing harm or danger?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I keep trying to separate it out, like maybe there’s some other bucket it belongs in, but the thing that brings me here is that same loop: if I miss one setting, one access rule, one warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything.\n\nAnd I know computers make everything sound dramatic—like, half the time a “critical alert” is just some software wanting attention, like a toddler with a keyboard—but in my head it doesn’t feel small. Even when part of me knows I’m being irrational, it feels like I’m personally responsible, like I can’t just move on unless I’m sure. So… no, I don’t think there are other thoughts that bother me in the same way. It’s mainly that.\n\nDoctor: Are your obsessive thoughts ever about something other than preventing harm or danger?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Or at least, not in the way that really gets stuck.\n\nIt’s always this same kind of thing—like I missed one setting, or an access rule, or some warning sign, and then somebody gets hurt or loses everything. And I know that sounds… I mean, computers have settings buried inside settings, it’s ridiculous, like you need a map just to find a checkbox sometimes—but when it hits me, it doesn’t feel like a little checkbox. It feels like if I don’t catch it, it’s on me personally.\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t say it’s about other stuff. It’s mostly that responsibility feeling, like I can’t move on unless I’m sure.\n\nDoctor: Are there any obsessive thoughts that you have which are not about preventing harm or danger, but still cause you significant distress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I keep circling back to the same thing, and I know I’ve probably said it a few different ways already.\n\nWhat brings me in is that thought that if I miss one setting, or one access rule, or some warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything. And it’s funny—not funny, but, you know, in my work there are always these tiny buried settings, and one menu leads to another menu, and sometimes the label doesn’t even mean what it sounds like it means. So I get stuck on that.\n\nEven though part of me knows it’s probably irrational, it doesn’t feel that way in the moment. It feels like I’m personally responsible, like if I move on without being sure, I’m basically allowing something terrible to happen. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No… not really. I mean, I get distressed, definitely, but it all seems tied back to that same thing.

It’s like, if I miss one setting, or one access rule, or some warning sign—there are so many little places things can hide in a system, honestly, it’s like whoever designs these menus has never met a normal human being—then someone could get hurt or lose everything. And part of me knows I’m probably taking it too far, but in the moment it doesn’t feel abstract. It feels like I’m personally responsible, like I can’t just close the laptop and move on unless I’m sure.

So I don’t think there’s another separate thought that bothers me like that. It’s mostly that.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I’m here because I can’t seem to get past these thoughts that if I miss one thing—like one setting, one access rule, one warning sign—then someone could get hurt or lose everything. I reread deployment plans and audit logs because I’m trying to make sure I didn’t miss the thing that causes some disaster later. Same with door locks, or even old messages sometimes, which sounds unrelated, but in my head it’s all like… evidence that I either did or didn’t catch something.\n\nI’ll count through the steps in my head until it feels safe or complete. And I know “safe” isn’t a real stopping point—I mean, at work we have actual criteria for that—but I’ll still stay at the office for hours after everyone else has left because if I stop too early, I panic, like I’m letting something bad happen.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about missing something or causing harm feel like they are coming from outside of your own thinking, or are they more like your own worries?\n\nPatient: No, it doesn’t feel like a voice or something outside me, if that’s what you mean. It feels like my own worry, my own thinking… just turned up way too loud.\n\nLike I’ll be looking at a setting or an access rule, and suddenly it’s, “If you miss this, someone could get hurt, or everything could fall apart,” and then I’m stuck there. And I know systems work always has risk—I mean, that’s the whole job, managing risk—but this feels different. It feels personal, like if I move on before I’m absolutely sure, then I’m responsible for whatever happens.\n\nSo… yeah, it’s coming from me. I know that. I just can’t make it feel less real in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Are these worries and repetitive behaviors limited to your work with systems or do they also involve other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: It’s not just work, no. I mean, work is where it gets the worst because there are actual systems and logs and deployment plans, and if something breaks, everyone knows, you know? So I’ll reread those over and over.\n\nBut it spills out into other stuff too. Door locks, old messages… I’ll go back and check them again and again, and then I’ll count through steps in my head until it feels “safe,” whatever that means. It’s like when I used to make these elaborate checklists for travel—sorry, that’s not really the point—but I get stuck in that same loop.\n\nAnd at the office I’ve stayed hours after everyone leaves because I can’t stop before it feels complete. If I try to just walk away, I panic.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about missing something or causing harm ever pop into your mind unexpectedly, without you intentionally thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes, they do. It’s not like I sit down and decide to worry about it. I’ll just be doing something—looking at a setting, or honestly sometimes not even actively working on it—and this thought kind of drops in: what if I missed one access rule, one warning sign, one tiny thing, and someone gets hurt or loses everything because of me?\n\nAnd then I know, on some level, that’s not a reasonable way to think about every single detail. I manage systems, not… I don’t know, it’s not like I’m personally holding the whole world together. But in the moment it feels that way. It hits like I’m responsible, and then I can’t just move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these repetitive behaviors or mental acts in response to your worries about causing harm in areas of your life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… outside work too. I mean, it’s worst at work, because I’ll reread deployment plans and audit logs over and over, and then I can lose hours that way—like, literally staying after everyone’s gone because if I stop before it feels complete, I start to panic.\n\nBut at home it’s similar, just with different things. Door locks, old messages… I’ll go back and check them again, and then again. Sometimes I’ll count through the steps in my head, like if I can get the sequence to feel right or “safe,” then maybe I can move on.\n\nIt sounds ridiculous saying it out loud. I used to be the person who made careful lists for trips and things—actually, my wife used to joke that I packed like I was planning a server migration—but this isn’t really that. It’s more like I’m trying to get this feeling to go away, and it doesn’t unless I check or mentally go through it again.\n\nDoctor: Are your repetitive checks or rituals specifically focused on preventing something bad from happening, or are they more about reducing a general feeling of anxiety or uncertainty?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of both, I think. Like, I tell myself I’m preventing something bad—if I reread the deployment plan one more time, or check the audit logs again, then I won’t miss the thing that causes a problem. Same with door locks or even old messages, like I’m making sure I didn’t leave something dangerous or wrong.\n\nBut if I’m honest, it’s also about the feeling. I’ll count through the steps in my head until they feel “safe,” which sounds… I don’t know, not very logical. And if I stop before it feels complete, I panic. I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone leaves doing that, rereading and checking, because walking away with that uncertainty feels unbearable.\n\nDoctor: Have these behaviors or rituals started to interfere with your relationships or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I mean, outside of work I used to have these routines that kept me, I don’t know, normal. Basketball with some guys, salsa nights with my wife and friends—well, she was always better at it than me, honestly, I mostly tried not to step on anyone—but I’ve stopped showing up. It’s like by the time I’m done checking or stuck trying to make a decision feel finished, I don’t have the time or the energy.\n\nAnd work has bled into it too. I missed this major cloud migration deadline because I just couldn’t sign off on decisions. I kept needing one more pass, one more confirmation. Now my team has started working around me instead of relying on me, which… that hurts. I used to be the person people came to for decisions. Now it feels like I’m becoming the bottleneck everywhere.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these thoughts and the need to repeat these actions cause you on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: It’s… a lot. Daily, honestly. What brings me in is that I keep getting these thoughts like, if I miss one setting, one access rule, one warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything. And I know, part of me knows that sounds extreme, because it’s not like every little checkbox is life or death, but in the moment it hits me like it is.\n\nIt’s like I’m personally responsible for catching every possible thing, and I can’t just move on from it. I’ll try to tell myself, okay, you checked, it’s fine, but then my brain goes, what if that was the one thing you missed? And then I’m right back in it. It’s exhausting. I mean, I used to be pretty good under pressure—my job kind of requires that—but this doesn’t feel like normal pressure anymore. It feels like I’m trapped in my own head most days.\n\nDoctor: Are your obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors only about preventing harm or are there other themes, such as symmetry or order, that also bother you?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s harm, I think. Like, it’s not really about things being lined up perfectly or the room needing to look a certain way—although my desk is a mess, which my wife used to joke was “organized chaos,” and honestly she wasn’t wrong.\n\nBut the thoughts that really grab me are the ones where I feel like if I miss one setting, one access rule, one warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything. And I know part of me understands that’s not always rational, but in the moment it feels like it’s on me personally, like I’m responsible for catching every possible thing. I can’t just let it go and move on.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other themes or concerns, besides preventing harm, that your obsessive thoughts or compulsive behaviors are focused on?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I keep trying to think if there’s some other category it fits into, but I don’t really notice another clear theme.\n\nI mean, I can get particular about things sometimes—my wife would say I overthink everything, including which takeout place to order from, and then the food gets cold while I’m reading reviews—but I don’t know if that’s the same thing you mean. The stuff that really hooks me, the stuff I can’t shake, seems to come back to preventing something bad from happening. Other than that, I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other themes or concerns, besides preventing harm, that your obsessive thoughts or compulsive behaviors are focused on?\n\nPatient: I mean, I keep circling back to the same thing, honestly. It’s not like I’m worried the pens aren’t straight or the dishes are in the wrong cabinet—though, actually, our kitchen cabinets are kind of a disaster because I unload the dishwasher in whatever way seems fastest, and then my wife can’t find the lids to anything.\n\nBut no, the thing that really grabs me is still that idea that if I miss one setting, one access rule, one warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything. And I know, sitting here, that sounds… too big, like my brain is making it bigger than it is. But when it hits, it feels like it’s my responsibility personally, and I can’t just move on from it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other themes or concerns, besides preventing harm, that you think about repeatedly or feel compelled to act on?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s another theme, at least not one I can really point to. I keep coming back to the same kind of thing, which is… if I miss one setting or one access rule or some warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything.\n\nAnd I know, when I say it out loud, it sounds extreme. Like, I used to manage systems where there were a hundred little permissions menus, and honestly half of them look like they were designed by someone who hates people. Sorry, that’s not really the point. The point is, even when part of me knows I’m probably making it bigger than it is, it hits me like it’s personally on me to catch it. Like if I move on, I’m choosing to let something bad happen. So I don’t really think it’s symmetry or order or anything like that—it’s that responsibility feeling.\n\nDoctor: Are there any obsessive thoughts that bother you which are not related to preventing harm or danger?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I keep trying to separate it out, like maybe there’s some other bucket it belongs in, but the thing that brings me here is that same loop: if I miss one setting, one access rule, one warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything.\n\nAnd I know computers make everything sound dramatic—like, half the time a “critical alert” is just some software wanting attention, like a toddler with a keyboard—but in my head it doesn’t feel small. Even when part of me knows I’m being irrational, it feels like I’m personally responsible, like I can’t just move on unless I’m sure. So… no, I don’t think there are other thoughts that bother me in the same way. It’s mainly that.\n\nDoctor: Are your obsessive thoughts ever about something other than preventing harm or danger?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Or at least, not in the way that really gets stuck.\n\nIt’s always this same kind of thing—like I missed one setting, or an access rule, or some warning sign, and then somebody gets hurt or loses everything. And I know that sounds… I mean, computers have settings buried inside settings, it’s ridiculous, like you need a map just to find a checkbox sometimes—but when it hits me, it doesn’t feel like a little checkbox. It feels like if I don’t catch it, it’s on me personally.\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t say it’s about other stuff. It’s mostly that responsibility feeling, like I can’t move on unless I’m sure.\n\nDoctor: Are there any obsessive thoughts that you have which are not about preventing harm or danger, but still cause you significant distress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I keep circling back to the same thing, and I know I’ve probably said it a few different ways already.\n\nWhat brings me in is that thought that if I miss one setting, or one access rule, or some warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything. And it’s funny—not funny, but, you know, in my work there are always these tiny buried settings, and one menu leads to another menu, and sometimes the label doesn’t even mean what it sounds like it means. So I get stuck on that.\n\nEven though part of me knows it’s probably irrational, it doesn’t feel that way in the moment. It feels like I’m personally responsible, like if I move on without being sure, I’m basically allowing something terrible to happen. So I don’t think there’s another separate kind of thought causing that level of anxiety. It’s that same responsibility thing.\n\nDoctor: Are there any obsessive thoughts that you experience which are not related to preventing harm, but still cause you significant distress?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I mean, I get distressed, definitely, but it all seems tied back to that same thing.\n\nIt’s like, if I miss one setting, or one access rule, or some warning sign—there are so many little places things can hide in a system, honestly, it’s like whoever designs these menus has never met a normal human being—then someone could get hurt or lose everything. And part of me knows I’m probably taking it too far, but in the moment it doesn’t feel abstract. It feels like I’m personally responsible, like I can’t just close the laptop and move on unless I’m sure.\n\nSo I don’t think there’s another separate thought that bothers me like that. It’s mostly that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F42.1", "F42.2"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

---------- turn 19 | patient:alignment | patient.build_alignment_messages() | model=gpt-5.5 ----------
========== INPUT [patient] ==========
[
  {

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think there’s another category of thought, if that’s what you mean.

What brings me in is still that same loop: I keep thinking if I miss one setting, one access rule, one warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything. And I know, on paper, that sounds irrational. I work with systems all day, and there are always settings nested under other settings, and honestly sometimes the interface is so bad you wonder if anyone tested it—but that’s not really the point.

The point is, when the thought hits, it feels like it’s on me personally. Like if I don’t catch it and something happens, I caused it by moving on too soon. So even if part of me knows it doesn’t make sense, I can’t just let it go. That’s the thing that causes the distress.

Which is ridiculous because safe isn’t really a measurable thing, you know? It feels like my own worry, my own thinking… just turned up way too loud.\n\nLike I’ll be looking at a setting or an access rule, and suddenly it’s, “If you miss this, someone could get hurt, or everything could fall apart,” and then I’m stuck there. And I know systems work always has risk—I mean, that’s the whole job, managing risk—but this feels different. It feels personal, like if I move on before I’m absolutely sure, then I’m responsible for whatever happens.\n\nSo… yeah, it’s coming from me. I know that. So I’ll reread those over and over.\n\nBut it spills out into other stuff too. Door locks, old messages… I’ll go back and check them again and again, and then I’ll count through steps in my head until it feels “safe,” whatever that means. It’s like when I used to make these elaborate checklists for travel—sorry, that’s not really the point—but I get stuck in that same loop.\n\nAnd at the office I’ve stayed hours after everyone leaves because I can’t stop before it feels complete. If I try to just walk away, I panic.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about missing something or causing harm ever pop into your mind unexpectedly, without you intentionally thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes, they do. It’s not like I sit down and decide to worry about it. I’ll just be doing something—looking at a setting, or honestly sometimes not even actively working on it—and this thought kind of drops in: what if I missed one access rule, one warning sign, one tiny thing, and someone gets hurt or loses everything because of me?\n\nAnd then I know, on some level, that’s not a reasonable way to think about every single detail. I manage systems, not… I don’t know, it’s not like I’m personally holding the whole world together. But in the moment it feels that way. It hits like I’m responsible, and then I can’t just move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these repetitive behaviors or mental acts in response to your worries about causing harm in areas of your life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… outside work too. I mean, it’s worst at work, because I’ll reread deployment plans and audit logs over and over, and then I can lose hours that way—like, literally staying after everyone’s gone because if I stop before it feels complete, I start to panic.\n\nBut at home it’s similar, just with different things. Door locks, old messages… I’ll go back and check them again, and then again. Sometimes I’ll count through the steps in my head, like if I can get the sequence to feel right or “safe,” then maybe I can move on.\n\nIt sounds ridiculous saying it out loud. I used to be the person who made careful lists for trips and things—actually, my wife used to joke that I packed like I was planning a server migration—but this isn’t really that. It’s more like I’m trying to get this feeling to go away, and it doesn’t unless I check or mentally go through it again.\n\nDoctor: Are your repetitive checks or rituals specifically focused on preventing something bad from happening, or are they more about reducing a general feeling of anxiety or uncertainty?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of both, I think. Like, I tell myself I’m preventing something bad—if I reread the deployment plan one more time, or check the audit logs again, then I won’t miss the thing that causes a problem. Same with door locks or even old messages, like I’m making sure I didn’t leave something dangerous or wrong.\n\nBut if I’m honest, it’s also about the feeling. I’ll count through the steps in my head until they feel “safe,” which sounds… I don’t know, not very logical. And if I stop before it feels complete, I panic. I’ve stayed at the office for hours after everyone leaves doing that, rereading and checking, because walking away with that uncertainty feels unbearable.\n\nDoctor: Have these behaviors or rituals started to interfere with your relationships or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. I mean, outside of work I used to have these routines that kept me, I don’t know, normal. Basketball with some guys, salsa nights with my wife and friends—well, she was always better at it than me, honestly, I mostly tried not to step on anyone—but I’ve stopped showing up. It’s like by the time I’m done checking or stuck trying to make a decision feel finished, I don’t have the time or the energy.\n\nAnd work has bled into it too. I missed this major cloud migration deadline because I just couldn’t sign off on decisions. I kept needing one more pass, one more confirmation. Now my team has started working around me instead of relying on me, which… that hurts. I used to be the person people came to for decisions. Now it feels like I’m becoming the bottleneck everywhere.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these thoughts and the need to repeat these actions cause you on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: It’s… a lot. Daily, honestly. What brings me in is that I keep getting these thoughts like, if I miss one setting, one access rule, one warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything. And I know, part of me knows that sounds extreme, because it’s not like every little checkbox is life or death, but in the moment it hits me like it is.\n\nIt’s like I’m personally responsible for catching every possible thing, and I can’t just move on from it. I’ll try to tell myself, okay, you checked, it’s fine, but then my brain goes, what if that was the one thing you missed? And then I’m right back in it. It’s exhausting. I mean, I used to be pretty good under pressure—my job kind of requires that—but this doesn’t feel like normal pressure anymore. It feels like I’m trapped in my own head most days.\n\nDoctor: Are your obsessive thoughts and compulsive behaviors only about preventing harm or are there other themes, such as symmetry or order, that also bother you?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s harm, I think. Like, it’s not really about things being lined up perfectly or the room needing to look a certain way—although my desk is a mess, which my wife used to joke was “organized chaos,” and honestly she wasn’t wrong.\n\nBut the thoughts that really grab me are the ones where I feel like if I miss one setting, one access rule, one warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything. And I know part of me understands that’s not always rational, but in the moment it feels like it’s on me personally, like I’m responsible for catching every possible thing. I can’t just let it go and move on.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other themes or concerns, besides preventing harm, that your obsessive thoughts or compulsive behaviors are focused on?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I keep trying to think if there’s some other category it fits into, but I don’t really notice another clear theme.\n\nI mean, I can get particular about things sometimes—my wife would say I overthink everything, including which takeout place to order from, and then the food gets cold while I’m reading reviews—but I don’t know if that’s the same thing you mean. The stuff that really hooks me, the stuff I can’t shake, seems to come back to preventing something bad from happening. Other than that, I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other themes or concerns, besides preventing harm, that your obsessive thoughts or compulsive behaviors are focused on?\n\nPatient: I mean, I keep circling back to the same thing, honestly. I keep coming back to the same kind of thing, which is… if I miss one setting or one access rule or some warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything.\n\nAnd I know, when I say it out loud, it sounds extreme. Like, I used to manage systems where there were a hundred little permissions menus, and honestly half of them look like they were designed by someone who hates people. Sorry, that’s not really the point. The point is, even when part of me knows I’m probably making it bigger than it is, it hits me like it’s personally on me to catch it. Like if I move on, I’m choosing to let something bad happen. So I don’t really think it’s symmetry or order or anything like that—it’s that responsibility feeling.\n\nDoctor: Are there any obsessive thoughts that bother you which are not related to preventing harm or danger?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I keep trying to separate it out, like maybe there’s some other bucket it belongs in, but the thing that brings me here is that same loop: if I miss one setting, one access rule, one warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything.\n\nAnd I know computers make everything sound dramatic—like, half the time a “critical alert” is just some software wanting attention, like a toddler with a keyboard—but in my head it doesn’t feel small. Even when part of me knows I’m being irrational, it feels like I’m personally responsible, like I can’t just move on unless I’m sure. So… no, I don’t think there are other thoughts that bother me in the same way. It’s mainly that.\n\nDoctor: Are your obsessive thoughts ever about something other than preventing harm or danger?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Or at least, not in the way that really gets stuck.\n\nIt’s always this same kind of thing—like I missed one setting, or an access rule, or some warning sign, and then somebody gets hurt or loses everything. It feels like if I don’t catch it, it’s on me personally.\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t say it’s about other stuff. It’s mostly that responsibility feeling, like I can’t move on unless I’m sure.\n\nDoctor: Are there any obsessive thoughts that you have which are not about preventing harm or danger, but still cause you significant distress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I keep circling back to the same thing, and I know I’ve probably said it a few different ways already.\n\nWhat brings me in is that thought that if I miss one setting, or one access rule, or some warning sign, then someone could get hurt or lose everything.
